Normalising Lustre Preserves Security
Sanjiva Prasad, R. Madhukar Yerraguntla

Abstract
The synchronous reactive data flow language Lustre is an expressive language, equipped with a suite of tools for modelling, simulating and model-checking a wide variety of safety-critical systems. A critical intermediate step in the formally certified compilation of Lustre involves translation to a well-behaved sub-language called “Normalised Lustre ” ( NLustre ). Recently, we proposed a simple Denning-style lattice-based secure information flow type system for NLustre , and proved its soundness by establishing that security-typed programs are non-interfering with respect to the co-inductive stream semantics. In this paper, we propose a similar security type system for unrestricted Lustre , and show that Bourke et al. ’s semantics-preserving normalisation transformations from Lustre to NLustre are security-preserving as well. A novelty is the use of refinement security types for node calls. The main result is the preservation of security types by the normalisation transformations. The soundness of our security typing rules is shown by establishing that well-security-typed programs are non-interfering, via a reduction to type-preservation (here), semantics-preservation (Bourke et al. ) and our previous result of non-interference for NLustre . Keywords : Synchronous reactive data flow, Lustre , Compiler transformation, Security type system, Non-interference, Security preservation.



1
Introduction

The synchronous reactive data flow language
Lustre

[
6
,
10
]
is an expressive language with an elegant formal semantics.
Its underlying deterministic, clocked model makes it a versatile programming paradigm, with diverse applications such as distributed embedded controllers, numerical computations, and complex Scade 6
[
7
]
safety-critical systems.
It is also equipped with a suite of tools, comprising:
(a) a certified compilation framework from the high-level model into lower-level imperative languages
[
2
,
3
]
;
(b) model-checkers
[
16
,
12
]

(c) simulation tools
[
11
]
for program development.

The development of a formally certified compiler from
Lustre
to an imperative language is the subject of active research
[
2
,
3
]
.
A critical intermediate step involves the translation from
Lustre
to a well-behaved sub-language called “Normalised
Lustre
” (
NLustre
), presented in
[
3
]
.
A recent paper (in French) defines the normalisation transformations from
Lustre
to
NLustre
, and establishes formally that they are semantics-preserving with respect to the stream semantics
[
4
]
(see Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
).

Recently we proposed a Denning-style lattice-based secure information flow (SIF) type system for
NLustre
, and proved its soundness by establishing that securely-typed programs are
non-interfering
with respect to the co-inductive stream semantics
[
15
]
.
The main ideas underlying the security type system are (i) that each stream is assigned (w.r.t. assumptions on variables) a
symbolic
security type,
(ii) equations induce constraints on security types of the defined variables and of the defining expressions, and
(iii) the output streams from a node have security levels at least as high as those of the input streams on which they depend.
The symbolic constraint-based formulations allows us to
infer
constraints that suffice to ensure security.
The rules are simple, intuitive and amenable to being incorporated into the mechanised certified compilation
[
14
]
already developed for
Lustre

[
5
]
.
In this paper, we propose a similar secure-information-flow type system for unrestricted
Lustre
(§
3
).
The main innovation is formulating symbolic constraint-based
refinement
(sub)types.
These are necessitated by the presence in
Lustre
of nested node calls (in
NLustre
, directed nesting is disallowed).

The security type system is shown by reduction to be
sound
with respect to
Lustre
’s
co-inductive
stream semantics.
While it is possible to do so directly by establishing that well-security-typed programs exhibit non-interference
[
9
]
using exactly the approach in
[
15
]
, here we do so via a sound
compiler transformation
:
We show that the semantics-preserving normalisation transformations (de-nesting and distribution, and explicit initialisation of
fby
) from
Lustre
to
NLustre
proposed in
[
4
]

preserve security types
as well (Theorem
4.1
in §
4
).
In particular, there is a strong correspondence at the level of
node definitions
.
The preservation of
security signatures
of node definitions is established via Lemma
3
in §
3
.
The main idea is to remove local variable types via a substitution procedure
simplify
(Figure
9
), showing that this maintains satisfiability of type constraints.
Since these transformations preserve operational behaviour
as well as security types of nodes
, and since we have already established non-interference for the target language
NLustre

[
15
, Theorem 5]
,
non-interference holds for source
Lustre
as well (Theorem
5.2
in §
5
).

Although the paper is intimately dependent on results of earlier work, we have endeavoured to keep it self-contained.
The reader interested in the complete stream semantics of
Lustre
may refer to the appendices.

Related Work.

We mention only the immediately relevant work here; a fuller discussion on related work can be found in
[
15
]
.
The formalisation of
Lustre
semantics and its certified compilation are discussed in detail in
[
1
,
2
,
3
]
.
The normalisation transformations examined here are proposed in
[
4
]
.
Our lattice-based SIF framework harks back to Denning’s seminal work
[
8
]
.
The idea of type systems for SIF can be found in
e.g.
,
[
18
]
.
That work also expressed soundness of a SIF type system in terms of the notion of non-interference
[
9
]
.
Our previous work
[
15
]
adapted that framework to a declarative data flow setting, showing that it is possible to
infer
minimal
partial-ordering constraints
between
symbolic types
.
The idea of type-preservation under the rewriting of programs is commonplace in logic and proof systems (“subject reduction”).

and
NLustre

\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\displaystyle d\hfil

(
program
)

Figure 3:
Common syntax of nodes and clocks

A
Lustre
program describes a synchronous network with
clocked
streams of data flowing between operators and
nodes
.
A program consists of a set of
node definitions
, each parameterised by clocked input and output flows.
A clock is a boolean stream – either a
base
clock or one
derived
from another clock when a variable takes a specific (boolean) value (
on

x=k
, where
k\in\{\texttt{T},\texttt{F}\}
).

Each node comprises a set of (possibly mutually recursive)
equations
, which define local variables and output flows in terms of flow
expressions
.
Such definitions are unique, and may appear in any order.

Lustre
satisfies the
definition
and
substitution
principles, namely that the context does not determine the meaning of an expression and that referential transparency holds.
Nodes do not have free variables.
Nodes cannot make recursive calls; therefore, the dependency order on nodes forms a DAG.
All expressions and equations can be
annotated
with a clock, following a static analysis to determine clock dependencies.

Figures
3
–
3

present the syntax of
Lustre
and
NLustre
.

Lustre
expressions (Figure
3
) include flows described by constants, variables, unary and binary operations on flows, as well as the flows obtained by sampling when a variable takes a particular boolean value (
when
), interpolation based on a boolean variable flow (
merge
), and conditional combinations of flows (
if_then_else
).
Of particular interest are flows involving guarded delays (
fby
) and those involving
node calls
.

NLustre
is a sub-language into which
Lustre
can be translated, from which subsequent compilation is easier.
The main differences between between
Lustre
and
NLustre
are
(i) the former supports
lists
of flows (written

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e\hfil

) for conciseness, whereas in the latter all flows are single streams;
(ii)
NLustre
requires that conditional and
merge
“control” expressions are not nested below unary and binary operators or sampling;
(iii) node call and delayed flows (
fby
) are treated as first-class expressions, whereas in
NLustre
, they can appear only in the context of equations;
(iv)
Lustre
permits nested node calls, whereas there is no nesting in
NLustre
;
(v) finally, the first argument of
fby
expressions in
NLustre
must be a
constant
, to enable a well-defined initialisation that can be easily implemented.

The
translation
from
Lustre
to
NLustre

[
4
]
involves
distributing
constructs over the individual components of lists of expressions, and
de-nesting
expressions by introducing fresh local variables (See §
4
).
The reader can see an example, adapted from
[
4
]
, of a
Lustre
program and its translation into
NLustre
in Figure
12
(ignoring for the moment the
security type annotations
therein).

2.1
Stream Semantics

The semantics of
Lustre
and
NLustre
programs are
synchronous
:
Each variable and expression defines a data stream which pulses with respect to a
clock
.
A clock is a stream of booleans (CompCert/Coq’s
true
and
false
in Vélus).
A flow takes its
n^{\textit{th}}
value on the
n^{\textit{th}}
clock tick,
i.e.
, some value, written
\text{\T1\guilsinglleft}{\color[rgb]{0.4375,0.5,0.4844}v}\text{\T1\guilsinglright}
, is present at instants when the clock value is
true
, and none (written
\text{\T1\guilsinglleft}\text{\T1\guilsinglright})
when it is
false
.
The
temporal operators

when
,
merge
and
fby
are used to express the complex clock-changing and clock-dependent behaviours of sampling, interpolation and delay respectively.

Formally the stream semantics is defined using predicates over the program graph
G
, a (co-inductive) stream
history
(
H_{*}:\textit{Ident}\rightarrow\textit{value}~\textit{Stream}
) that associates value streams to variables, and a clock
bs

[
3
,
15
,
4
]
.
Semantic operations on (lists of) streams are written in
blue
sans serif
typeface.
Streams are written in
red
, with lists of streams usually written in
bold face
.
All these stream operators, defined co-inductively, enforce the clocking regime, ensuring the presence of a value when the clock is
true
, and absence when
false
.

The predicate
G,H_{*},bs~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\vdash}~e\Downarrow_{\textsf{e}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{es}}}
relates an
expression

e
to a
list
of streams, written
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{es}}
.
A list consisting of only a single stream
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}es}
is explicitly denoted as
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}[es]}
.
The semantics of
equations
are expressed using the predicate

G,H_{*},bs~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}~\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle eq_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle eq_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle eq_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle eq_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}
, which requires
consistency
between the assumed and defined stream histories in
H_{*}
for the program variables, as induced by the equations.
Finally, the semantics of
nodes
is given as a stream history transformer predicate

G~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{0.38}[0.5]{$\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68744pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{-6.26666pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle s}$}}}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle}$}}}$}}}$}\,}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}}})\Searrow{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}}.

We discuss here only some constructs which relate to the normalisation transformations.
Appendices
0.B
and
0.C
present a complete account of the stream semantics for
Lustre
and
NLustre
, consistent with
[
5
]
.

\mathinner{\raise 2.5pt\vtop{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 20.50067pt\hbox{$\penalty H_{*}(x)={{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}xs}}$}}\kern 20.31006pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule width=89.1749pt,height=3.38501pt,depth=3.38501pt\vbox{\kern-5.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{~(\footnotesize{LSvar})}}\kern-5.0pt}}}}\kern 20.31006pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$G,H_{*},bs~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\vdash}~x\Downarrow_{\textsf{e}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}[xs]}$}}}}

Rule (LSvar) associates a variable
x
to the stream given by
H_{*}(x)
.

\mathinner{\raise 2.5pt\vtop{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 12.46056pt\hbox{$\penalty\vbox{\halign{\hfil{\m@th$#\null$}&{\m@th$\null#$}\hfil\cr\forall i:~~G,H_{*},bs~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\vdash}~e0_{i}\Downarrow_{\textsf{e}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{e0s}}_{i}}~~~~\forall j:~G,H_{*},bs~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\vdash}~e_{j}\Downarrow_{\textsf{e}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{es}}_{j}}\hbox{}\cr\kern 4.2679pt\cr~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{\textsf{fby}_{L}}}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}({\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\flat}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern A delay operation is implemented by
e0~\texttt{fby}~e
.
The rule (LSfby) is to be read as follows.
Let each expression
e0_{i}
denote a list of streams

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{e0s_{i}}}
, and each expression
e_{j}
denote a list of streams
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{es_{j}}}
.
The predicate
\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{fby}}_{L}}

maps
the predicate
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{fby}}_{L}
to act on the corresponding components of
lists
of streams,
i.e.
,

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{\textsf{fby}_{L}}}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{zs}}}}~\textrm{abbreviates}~\bigwedge_{i\in[1,m]}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{fby}_{L}}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}xs_{i}}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}ys_{i}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}zs_{i}}}

(Similarly for the predicates

\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{when}}}
,

\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{merge}}}
, and
\widehat{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{ite}}}
.)
The operation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\flat}(\_)
flattens a list of lists (of possibly different lengths) into a single list.
Flattening is required since expression
e_{i}
may in general denote a
list
of streams
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{es}}_{i}
.
The output list of streams
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{os}}
consists of streams whose first elements are taken from each stream in
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\flat}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{e0s}}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{e0s}}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{e0s}}_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern with the rest taken from the corresponding component of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\flat}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern .

according to the history

with the corresponding components of the tuple of streams
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\flat}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern The rule (LSndef) presents the meaning given to the definition named
f
of a node
g\in G
as a stream list transformer.
If history
H_{*}
assigns lists of streams to the input and output variables for a node in a manner such that the semantics of the equations in the node are satisfied, then the semantic function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}
transforms input stream list
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}
to output stream list
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}
.
The operation
base-of
finds an appropriate base clock with respect to which a given list of value streams pulse.

0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle e_{i}\hfil$\crcr}}})\Downarrow_{\textsf{e}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}$}}}}

The rule (LSncall) applies the stream transformer semantic function
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}
to the stream list
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}
corresponding to the tuple of arguments

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle e_{i}\hfil

, and returns the stream list
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}
.

Stream semantics for
NLustre
.

The semantic relations for
NLustre
are either identical to (as in constants, variables, unary and binary operations) or else the singleton cases of the rules for
Lustre
(as in
merge
,
ite
,
when
).
The main differences are in the occurrence of
fby
(now in a restricted form) and node call only in the context of equations, (which are clock-annotated).

Figure 4:
Stream semantics of
NLustre
nodes and equations

The (NSfby’) rule for
fby
in an equational context uses the semantic operation
\textsf{fby}_{NL}
, which differs from
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{fby}}_{L}
in that it requires its first argument to be a constant rather than a stream.
The (NSndef’) rule only differs from (LSndef) in that after clock alignment during
transcription
, we have an additional requirement of
H_{*}
being in accordance with the base clock
bs
, enforced by
respects-clock
.

Finally, the rule rule (NSncall’) for node call, now in an equational context, is similar to (LSncall) except that it constrains the clock modulating the equation to be the base clock of the input flows.

3
A Security Type System for
Lustre

We define a symbolic secure information flow type system, where under security-level type assumptions for program variables,
Lustre
expressions are given a
security type
, and
Lustre
equations induce a set of
ordering constraints
over security types.

Security type expressions (
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha,\beta}
) for
Lustre
are either (i)
type variables
(written
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta})
drawn from a set
STV
, or (ii) constructed using a
join
operator (written
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\beta}
).
(iii) The identity element of the associative, commutative and idempotent monoid operation
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqcup}
is
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot}
.
While the above suffice for
NLustre
, for
Lustre
we introduce (iv)
refinement types

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|\rho|\!\}}
, where symbolic constraint
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
modulates type expression
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}
.
Constraints on security types, typically
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
, are (conjunctions of) relations of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\beta}
.
Since security types are to be interpreted with respect to a complete lattice, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\beta}
exactly when

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\beta~=~\beta}
.
Our proposed security types and their equational theory are presented in Figure
5
.
The security types for
NLustre
and their equational theory
[
15
]
are highlighted in grey within the diagram.
This congruence on
NLustre
types (henceforth
\equiv_{NL}
), which is given in the highlighted second line of Figure
5
, is significantly simpler since it does not involve refinement types!

Types:

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\theta}~::=\hbox{\pagecolor{mygrey}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot}$}\;|\;\hbox{\pagecolor{mygrey}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta}\in\textit{STV}$}\;|\;\hbox{\pagecolor{mygrey}$\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\beta}$}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|\rho|\!\}}

Constraints:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}~::=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}(\theta\sqsubseteq\alpha)}^{*}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}(\alpha\sqcup\beta)\sqcup\theta}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup(\beta\sqcup\theta)},~\hfill~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\alpha}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha},~\hfill~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\beta}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta\sqcup\alpha},~\hfill~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\bot}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot\sqcup\alpha},

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!||\!\}}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha},~~\hfill~~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1}\{\!|\rho_{1}|\!\}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}~\sqcup}~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{2}\{\!|\rho_{2}|\!\}}~=~({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1}\sqcup\alpha_{2}}){\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\{\!|}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}|\!\}},~~\hfill~~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|\rho_{1}|\!\}\{\!|\rho_{2}|\!\}}~=~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}|\!\}}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern ,

\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|\rho_{1}|\!\}\sqsubseteq\beta\{\!|\rho_{2}|\!\}}\}~=~\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\beta}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{2}}

Figure 5:
Security types, constraints and their properties

We write
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha[\theta_{i}/\delta_{i}]}
for
i=1,\ldots,k
to denote the (simultaneous) substitution of security types
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\theta_{i}}
for security type variables
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{i}}
in security type
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}
.
The notation extends to substitutions on tuples (

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil

) and constraints (
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\beta}
).

Constraints are interpreted in a security class lattice
SC
by the homomorphic extension of a ground instantiation
s:\textit{STV}\rightarrow\textit{SC}
, such that

s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot})=\bot
,

s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqcup\beta})=s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha})\sqcup s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta})
,

s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}})=\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{i}})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{i}})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{i}})\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{i}})\hfil$\crcr}}}
,

s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\beta})=s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha})\sqsubseteq s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta})
, and

s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|\rho|\!\}})=s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha})
if
s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho})
holds in
SC
,
i.e.
, if “
s
satisfies
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
” (else undefined).
To make sense, refinement type
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\{\!|\rho|\!\}}
requires the satisfaction of constraint
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
.

Lemma 1
(Soundness)

The equational theory induced by the equalities in Figure
5
is sound with respect to any ground instantiation
s
, i.e.,
(i)
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha=\beta}
implies
s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha})=s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta})
, and (ii)
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}={\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{2}}
implies
s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}})
is satisfied iff
s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{2}})
is.

Lemma 2
(Confluence)

All equations other than those of associativity and commutativity (AC) can be oriented left-to-right into rewriting rules.
The rewriting system is confluent modulo AC.
Equal types (respectively, equal constraints) can be rewritten to a common form modulo AC.

Proof sketch.
The equational theory
\equiv_{NL}
is decidable, since it is a convergent rewriting system modulo AC.
The rules in lines 3 and 4 can all be oriented left to right.
We use completion
[
13
]
to introduce rule

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1}\{\!|\rho_{1}|\!\}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}~\sqcup}~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{2}}\longrightarrow({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1}\sqcup\alpha_{2}}){\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\{\!|}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}|\!\}}
, when
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{2}}
is not a refinement type.
We use the theory of strongly coherent rewriting modulo AC
[
17
]
, to efficiently decide type equality.

\square
.

3.1
Security Typing Rules

Assume typing environment
\Gamma:\textit{Ident}\rightarrow\textit{ST}
, a partial function associating a security type to each free variable
x
in a
Lustre
program phrase.
Expressions and clocks are type-checked with the predicates:

\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle e}}{{\vdash}}}e:{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle\alpha\hfil$\crcr}}}}

and

\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ck}}{{\vdash}}}ck:{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}
respectively.
These are read as “under the context
\Gamma
mapping variables to security types,
e
,
ck
have security type(s)
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}
”.
The types for tupled expressions are tuples of the types of the component expressions.
For equations, we use the predicate:

\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle eqn}}{{\vdash}}eq\texttt{~:>}~{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}}
,
which states that under the context
\Gamma
, equation
eq
when type-elaborated generates constraints
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
.
Elementary constraints for equations are of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha\sqsubseteq\beta}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}
is the security type of the defined variable, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}
the security type obtained from that of the defining expression joined with the clock’s security type.
Since every flow in
Lustre
is defined
exactly once
, by the Definition Principle, no further security constraints apply.

The security typing rules for
Lustre
are presented in Figures
6
–
8
, plus the rules for node definition and node call.
These rules generalise those in
[
15
]
to handle expressions representing lists of flows, and nested node calls.
The rules for
NLustre
expressions other than node call and
fby
are just the singleton cases.
Node call and
fby
are handled by the rule for equations.

\begin{array}[]{c}\mathinner{\raise 2.5pt\vtop{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma(\texttt{base})={\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}$}}\kern 20.31006pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule width=50.76045pt,height=3.38501pt,depth=3.38501pt\vbox{\kern-5.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{~(\footnotesize{LTbase})}}\kern-5.0pt}}}}\kern 20.31006pt\hbox{\kern 0.72525pt\hbox{$\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ck}}{{\vdash}}}\texttt{base}:{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}$}}}}~~~~~~\mathinner{\raise 2.5pt\vtop{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 6.82974pt\hbox{$\penalty\Gamma(x)={\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma_{1}}~~\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ck}}{{\vdash}}}ck:{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma_{2}}$}}\kern 20.31006pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\vrule width=105.70377pt,height=3.38501pt,depth=3.38501pt\vbox{\kern-5.0pt\hbox{\kern 1.0pt\hbox{~(\footnotesize{LTon})}}\kern-5.0pt}}}}\kern 20.31006pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.06,0.46,1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle ck}}{{\vdash}}}ck~\texttt{on}~x=k:{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma_{1}\sqcup\gamma_{2}}$}}}}\\
\end{array}

security typing rules for clocks

Figure 8:
Lustre
security typing rules for equations

In (LTbase), we assume
\Gamma
maps the base clock
base
to some security variable (
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}
by convention).
In (LTon), the security type of the derived clock is the join of the security types of the clock
ck
and that of the variable
x
.

Constants have security type
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot}
, irrespective of the context (rule (LTcnst)).
For variables, in rule (LTvar), we look up their security type in the context
\Gamma
.
Unary operations preserve the type of their arguments (rule (LTunop)).
Binary (
\oplus
,
when
and
fby
) and ternary (
if-then-else
and
merge
) operations on flows generate a flow with a security type that is the join of the types of the operand flows (rules (LTbinop), (LTwhn), (LTmrg), (LTite), and (LTfby).
In operations on
lists of flows
, the security types are computed component-wise.
There is an implicit dependency on the security level of the common clock of the operand flows for these operators.
This dependence on the security level of the clock is made explicit in the rule for equations.
In general, the security type for any constructed expression is the join of those of its components (and of the clock).

Node call.

Node calls assume that we have a security signature for the node definition (described below).
We can then securely type node calls by instantiating the security signature with the types of the actual arguments (and that of the base clock).
Note the rule (LTncall) creates refinement types consisting of the output types
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta_{i}}
modulated with
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho^{\prime}}
, the instantiated set of constraints
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
taken from the node signature:

Node definition.

A node definition is given a signature

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Node}}{{\vdash}}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0,0.332}Node} f }{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}})^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}}
, which is to be read as saying that the node named
f
relates the security types

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil

of the input variables (and
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}
, that of the base clock) to the types of the output variables

\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr

\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil

, via the constraints

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{n},\delta_{1},\dots\delta_{k},\beta_{1},\dots\beta_{m},\gamma}
be distinct
fresh type variables
.
Assume these to be the types of the input, local and output variables, and that of the base clock.
We compute the constraints over these variables induced by the nodes equations.
Finally, we eliminate, via substitution, the type variables given to the local program variables, since these should not appear in the node’s interface.
The security signature of a node definition is thus given as:

\mathinner{\raise 2.5pt\vtop{\hbox{\vbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{$\penalty\vbox{\halign{\hfil{\m@th$#\null$}&{\m@th$\null#$}\hfil\cr The node signature (and call) rules can be formulated in this step-wise and modular manner since
Lustre
does not allow recursive node calls and cyclic dependencies.
Further, all variables in a node definition are explicitly accounted for as input and output parameters or local variables, so no extra contextual information is required.

Figure 9:
Eliminating local variables’ security type constraints

Observe that in the (LTndef) rule,
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{i}}
are fresh security type variables assigned to the local variables in a node.
Since there will be exactly one defining equation for any local variable
z_{i}
, note that in constraints
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho^{\prime}}
, there will be exactly one constraint in which
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{i}}
is on the right, and this is of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\delta_{i}}
.
Procedure
simplify
(Figure
9
) serially (in some arbitrary but fixed order) eliminates such type variables via substitution in the types and type constraints.
Our definition of
simplify
here generalises that given for the types of
NLustre
in
[
15
]
.

Lemma 3
(Correctness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\textsf{simplify}}~({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern )

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
be a set of constraints such that for a security type variable
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta}
, there is at most one constraint of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mu\sqsubseteq\delta}
.
Let
s
be a ground instantiation of security type variables in a security class lattice
SC
such that
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
is satisfied by
s
.

1.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}={\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu\sqsubseteq\delta}\}
, where variable
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta}
is not in
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu}/{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta}]
is satisfied by
s
. (Assume disjoint union.)

2.

If
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}={\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu\sqcup\delta\sqsubseteq\delta}\}
, where variable
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta}
is not in
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{1}}[{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\nu}/{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta}]
is satisfied by
s
. (Assume disjoint union.)

Lemma
3
is central to establishing that the type signature of a node does not change in the normalisation transformations of §
4
, which introduce equations involving fresh local program variables.

Revisiting Figure
12
, the reader can see the type system at work, with the
security types and constraints

annotated.
Also shown is the simplification of constraints using
simplify
.

4
Normalisation

\\

&&\textrm{in}~([\diamond~e^{\prime}_{1}]^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}},eqs^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}})\\

Figure 10:
Lustre
to
NLustre
normalisation

We now present Bourke
et al.
’s “normalisation transformations” , which de-nest and distribute operators over lists (tuples) of expressions, and finally transform
fby
expressions to a form where the first argument is a constant.

Normalising an
n
-tuple of
Lustre
expressions yields an
m
-tuple of
Lustre
expressions without tupling and nesting, and a set of equations, defining fresh local variables (Figure
10
).
We denote the transformation as

([e^{\prime}_{1},\ldots,e^{\prime}_{m}]^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{m}}},eqs^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}})\leftarrow\lfloor e_{1},...,e_{n}\rfloor

where we have additionally decorated the transformations of
[
4
]
with security types for each member of the tuple of expressions, and with a set of type constraints for the generated equations.
We show that the normalisation transformations are indeed
typed transformations
.
Our type annotations indicate why security types and constraints of well-security-typed
Lustre
programs are preserved (modulo satisfaction), as in Theorem
4.1
.

The rules
(Xcnst)
-
(Xbinop)
for constants, variables, unary and binary operators are obvious, generating no new equations.
In rule
(Xwhn)
, where the sampling condition is distributed over the members of the tuple, the security type for each expression is obtained by taking a join of the security type
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{i}}
of the expression
e^{\prime}_{i}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}
,
i.e.
, that of the variable
x
.

Of primary interest are the rules
(Xfby)
for
fby
and
(Xncall)
for
node call
, where fresh variables
x_{i}
and their defining equations are introduced.
In these cases, we introduce
fresh
security type variables
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{i}}
for the
x_{i}
, and add appropriate constraints.
The rules
(Xite)
and
(Xmrg)
resemble
(Xfby)
in most respects.
In rule
(Xncall)
, the constraints are obtained from the node signature via substitution.

The rules
(Xbase)
and
(Xon)
for clocks also introduce no equations.
The rules
(Xtup)
for tuples (lists) of expressions and
(Xeqs)
for equations regroup the resulting expressions appropriately.
The translation of node definitions involves translating the equations, and adding the fresh local variables.

\lfloor x^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\theta}}=_{ck^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}e_{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}}~\texttt{fby}_{l}~e^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}}\rfloor_{\textit{fby}}=\begin{cases}xinit^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{1}}}=_{ck^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}\texttt{true}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot}}~\texttt{fby}_{nl}~\texttt{false}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot}}~\hfill~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot\sqcup\gamma~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}~\delta_{1}}\\
px^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{2}}}=_{ck^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}c^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\bot}}~\texttt{fby}_{nl}~e^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}}~\hfill~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma\sqcup\beta~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}~\delta_{2}}\\
x^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\theta}}=_{ck^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}\texttt{if}~{xinit^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{1}}}}\texttt{then}~{e_{0}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}}}~\hfill~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma\sqcup\delta_{1}\sqcup\alpha\sqcup\delta_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}~\theta}\\
~~~~~~~~~~~\texttt{else}~{px^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta_{2}}}}\end{cases}

Explicit
fby
initialisation

and for any ground instantiation
s
,

s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho})
implies
s({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho^{\prime}})
.

The proof is on the DAG structure of
G
.
Here we rely on the modularity of nodes, and the correctness of
simplify
(Lemma
3
).
The proof employs induction on the structure of expressions.
For the further explicit initialisation of
fby
(Figure
11
), the preservation of security via
simplify
is easy to see.

Semantics preservation.

We recall the important results from
[
4
]
, which establish the preservation of stream semantics by the transformations.

Theorem 4.2
(Preservation of semantics. Theorem 2 of
[
4
]
)

De-nesting and distribution preserve the semantics of
Lustre
programs.
(La passe de désimbrication et distributivité préserve la sémantique des programmes.)

\forall G~f~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}:~~~{G~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{0.38}[0.5]{$\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68744pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{-6.26666pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle s}$}}}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle}$}}}$}}}$}\,}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}}})\Searrow{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}}}\implies{\lfloor G\rfloor~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{0.38}[0.5]{$\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68744pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{-6.26666pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle s}$}}}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle}$}}}$}}}$}\,}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}}})\Searrow{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}}}

Theorem 4.3
(Preservation of semantics. Theorem 3 of
[
4
]
)

The explicit initialisations of
fby
preserve the semantics of the programs.
(L’explicitation des initialisations préserve la sémantique des programmes.)

\forall G~f~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}~{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}:~~~{G~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{0.38}[0.5]{$\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68744pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{-6.26666pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle s}$}}}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle}$}}}$}}}$}\,}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}}})\Searrow{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}}}\implies{\lfloor G\rfloor_{fby}~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\,\scalebox{0.38}[0.5]{$\mathrel{\hbox{\raisebox{3.44444pt}{$\rule[-6.45831pt]{0.47787pt}{12.91663pt}\rule[-0.23894pt]{8.68744pt}{0.47787pt}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{-6.26666pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle s}$}}}\hskip-8.68744pt\raisebox{1.96112pt}{\makebox[8.68744pt]{\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\textstyle}$}}}$}}}$}\,}~{{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\widehat{f}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{xs}}}})\Searrow{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathbf{ys}}}}}

4.1
Example

We adapt an example from
[
4
]
to illustrate the translation and security-type preservation.
The
re_trig
node in Figure
12
uses the
cnt_dn
node (Figure
21
) to implement a count-down timer that is explicitly triggered whenever there is a rising edge (represented by
edge
) on
i
.
If the count
v
expires to
0
before a
T
on
i
, the counter isn’t allowed restart the count.
Output
o
represents an active count in progress.

We annotate the program with
security types
(superscripts)
and
constraints
for each equation (as comments), according to the typing rules.

cnt_dn
is assumed to have security signature

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Node}}{{\vdash}}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0,0.332}Node} {cnt\_dn} }{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}({\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}})^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}\xrightarrow{\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma\sqcup\alpha_{1}\sqcup\alpha_{2}~{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}~\beta}\}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}}.

Eliminating the security types
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{2}},{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{3}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{6}}
,
of the local variables
edge
,
c
,
v
and nested call to
cnt_dn
respectively, we
get the constraint

\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma^{\prime}\sqcup\alpha^{\prime}_{1}\sqcup\alpha^{\prime}_{2}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\sqsubseteq}\beta^{\prime}}\}
.

Normalisation introduces local variables (
v21,v22,v24
) with security types

{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{4}},{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{5}},{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{6}}
.
(Identical names have been used to show the correspondence.)
The
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{i}}
are eliminated by
simplify
, and the refinement type
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\delta^{\prime}_{6}\{\!|\rho^{\prime}|\!\}}
for the node call in the
Lustre
version becomes an explicit constraint
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho_{5}}
in
NLustre
.
Observe that the security signature remains the same across the translation.

Lemma
4
relates the satisfaction of constraints on security types generated during a node call to satisfaction in a security lattice via a ground instantiation.
Again we rely on the modularity of nodes — that no recursive calls are permitted, and nodes do not have free variables.

Definition 1
(Node Security;
Definition III.1 in
[
15
]
)

Let
g
be a node in the program graph
G
with security signature

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Node}}{{\vdash}}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0,0.332}Node} f }{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}(\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}})^{\gamma}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}}.

Let
s
be a ground instantiation that maps the security type variables in the set

\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}(\alpha_{1},\dots\alpha_{n})}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}(\beta_{1},\dots\beta_{m})}\}\cup\{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}\}

to the security class lattice
SC
.

Node
g
is secure with respect to
s
if
(i)
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
is satisfied by
s
;
(ii) For each node
g^{\prime}
on which
g
is directly dependent,
g^{\prime}
is secure with respect to the appropriate ground instantiations for each call to
g^{\prime}
in
g
as given by Lemma
4
.

This definition captures the intuition of node security in that all the constraints generated for the equations within the node must be satisfied, and that each internal node call should also be secure.

The notion of non-interference requires limiting observation to streams whose security level is at most a given security level
t
.

Definition 2
(
(\sqsubseteq t)
-projected Stream; Definition IV.1 in
[
15
]
)

Suppose
t\in\textit{SC}
is a security class.
Let
X
be a set of program variables,
\Gamma
be security type assumptions for variables in
X
, and
s
be a ground instantiation,
i.e.,
\Gamma\circ s
maps variables in
X
to security classes in

SC
.
Let us define
X_{\sqsubseteq t}=\{x\in X~|~(\Gamma\circ s)(x)\sqsubseteq t\}
.
Let
H_{*}
be a Stream history such that
X\subseteq\textit{dom}(H_{*})
.
Define
H_{*}|_{X_{\sqsubseteq t}}
as the projection of
H_{*}
to
X_{\sqsubseteq t}
,
i.e.
, restricted to those variables that are at security level
t
or lower:

H_{*}|_{X_{\sqsubseteq t}}(x)=H_{*}(x)~~~~\mbox{for $x\in X_{\sqsubseteq t}$}.

Theorem 5.1
(Non-interference for
NLustre
; Theorem 5 in
[
15
]
)

Let
g\in G
be a node with security signature

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Node}}{{\vdash}}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0.4961,0,0.332}Node} f }{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\alpha}\hfil$\crcr}}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\gamma}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}}{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\mathchoice{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\displaystyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\textstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\textstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}{\vbox{\halign{#\cr\kern-0.7pt\cr$\mkern 2.0mu\scriptscriptstyle\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraitd}$}}}\mkern-1.5mu\leaders{\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle\mkern 0.0mu\mathrel{\mathchoice{{}\hbox{$\displaystyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\textstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\montraita}$}}{{}\hbox{$\scriptscriptstyle{\montraita}$}}}\mkern 0.0mu$}}{\hfill}\mkern-1.5mu\fldr$\crcr\kern-0.3pt\cr$\hfil\scriptscriptstyle{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\beta}\hfil$\crcr}}}}

which is secure with respect to ground instantiation
s
of the type variables.

Let
eqs
be the set of equations in
g
.
Let
X=fv(eqs)-dv(eqs)
, i.e., the input variables in
eqs
.

Let
V=fv(eqs)\cup dv(eqs)
, i.e., the input, output and local variables.

Let
\Gamma
(and
s
) be such that

\Gamma\stackrel{{\scriptstyle eqn}}{{\vdash}}eqs\texttt{~:>}~{{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0.1641,0,1}\rho}
is satisfied by
s
.
Let
t\in\textit{SC}
be any security level.
Let
bs
be a given (base) clock stream.

Let
H_{*}
and
H_{*}^{\prime}
be such that

1.

for all
eq\in eqs
:
G,H_{*},bs~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}~eq

and
G,H_{*}^{\prime},bs~{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\vdash}~eq
, i.e., both
H_{*}
and
H_{*}^{\prime}
are consistent Stream histories on each of the equations.

2.

H_{*}|_{X_{\sqsubseteq t}}=H_{*}^{\prime}|_{X_{\sqsubseteq t}}
, i.e.,

H_{*}
and
H_{*}^{\prime}
agree on the input variables which are at a security level
t
or below.

Then
H_{*}|_{V_{\sqsubseteq t}}=H_{*}^{\prime}|_{V_{\sqsubseteq t}}
, i.e,
H_{*}
and
H_{*}^{\prime}
agree on all variables of the node that are given a security level
t
or below.

Theorem 5.2
(Non-interference for
Lustre
)

If program
G
is well-security-typed in
Lustre
, then it exhibits non-interference with respect to
Lustre
’s stream semantics.

Proof sketch.
Let
G
be well-security-typed in
Lustre
.
This means that each node
g\in G
is well-security-typed.
By induction on the DAG structure of
G
, using Theorem
4.1
,
\lfloor G\rfloor
is well-security-typed.
By Theorem
5.1
,
\lfloor G\rfloor
exhibits non-interference.
By Theorems
4.2
and
4.3
,
\lfloor G\rfloor
and
G
have the same
extensional
semantics for each node.
Therefore,
G
exhibits non-interference.

6
Conclusions

We have presented a novel security type system for
Lustre
using the notion of constraint-based refinement (sub)types.
Using security-type preservation and earlier results, we have shown its semantic soundness, expressed in terms of non-interference, with respect to the language’s stream semantics.

We are developing mechanised proofs of these results, which can be integrated into the Velús verified compiler framework
[
5
]
.

While
Lustre
’s value type system is quite jejune, this security type system is not.
It is therefore satisfying to see that it satisfies a subject reduction property
1
1

1

At SYNCHRON 2020, De Simone asked Jeanmaire and Pesin whether the terminology “normalisation” used in their work
[
4
]
was related in any way to notions of normalisation seen in,
e.g.
, the
\lambda
-calculus. It is!
.
A difficult aspect encountered during the transcription phase
[
4
]
concerns alignment of clocks in the presence of complex clock dependencies.
We clarify that our type system, being static, only considers
security levels of clocks
, not actual clock behaviour, and therefore is free from such complications.
Further, the clocks induce no timing side-channels since the typing rules enforce,
a fortiori
, that the security type of any (clocked) expression is at least as high as that of its clock.

and

Free and defined variables for equations

Appendix 0.B
Stream Semantics

We present here a specification of core
Lustre
’s co-inductive stream semantics, with some commentary and intuition.
This consolidates various earlier presentations of rules
[
2
,
3
,
15
,
4
]
, and can be seen as an abstract Coq-independent specification of the semantics encoded in the Vélus development.
